Raising questions or concerns
Lenovo is committed to fostering a speak up culture,
where employees, contractors and business partners
are empowered to speak up on anything that appears
unethical, illegal, or suspicious. Lenovo has established
clear processes and various reporting channels for
raising questions or reporting concerns. Employees
are encouraged to raise concerns to their managers,
Human Resources, the ECO, Internal Audit, or the Legal
Department about any potential issues including, but not
limited to, those known about or suspected:
•	 Fraud	by	or	against	Lenovo
•	 Bribery	or	Corruption
•	 Unethical	business	conduct
•	 Violation	of	legal	or	regulatory	requirements
•	 Substantial	and	specific	danger	to	health	and	safety
•	 Violation	of	Lenovo’s	corporate	policies	and
guidelines, particularly the Code of Conduct
Intellectual Property
Lenovo values intellectual property as it innovates for the
future. Lenovo expects employees to protect intellectual
property and to respect the intellectual property rights
of other companies and individuals. Lenovo secures
its intellectual property by using patents, copyrights,
trademarks, confidential information, related contract
rights, and other applicable forms of legal protection.
Trade Compliance
Lenovo is fully committed to complying with all applicable
global trade laws and regulations in the markets where it
conducts business. This includes adherence to customs
and import regulations, export controls, economic
sanctions, and anti-boycott regulations as mandated by
relevant authorities in each jurisdiction.
All employees are expected to uphold Lenovo’s Code
of Conduct and Global Trade Compliance Policy, which
define the requirements for maintaining compliance
with these laws and regulations. Lenovo also requires its
business partners to comply with applicable global trade
laws and regulations pursuant to its Partner Code of
Conduct.
Additionally, Lenovo is Customs-Trade Partnership Against
Terrorism (CTPAT) certified and a member of the CTPAT
Trade Compliance program, demonstrating Lenovo’s
commitment to supply chain security and regulatory
compliance.
Privacy & Data Protection
Lenovo recognizes the great importance of privacy to
individuals everywhere — customers, website visitors,
product users, employees — everyone. The responsible use
and protection of personal and other information under
Lenovo’s care is a core value. To ensure adherence to
Lenovo’s privacy and data protection policies, principles,
and processes, Lenovo maintains a global Privacy & Data
Protection Program led by the Legal Department and a
cross-functional Privacy Working Group comprised of
key partners drawn from Information Security, Product
Security, Product Development, Marketing, E-Commerce,
Service and Repair, Human Resources, and other groups.
Key elements of Lenovo’s approach to responsibly using
and protecting information of concern include:
•	 Monitoring	privacy	and	data	protection	legal	and
regulatory trends, advising senior leadership, and
improving Lenovo’s privacy and data protection
practices
•	 Harmonizing	global	privacy	and	data	protection
requirements into a corporate-wide Privacy
Statement, Privacy Standard, and set of Guiding
Privacy Principles intended to drive how Lenovo
handles personal, user, device and certain other
identifiable information, including developing and
updating Lenovo privacy controls and processes

With operations and supply chains that extend around the
world, Lenovo is uniquely positioned to support the global
collective impact of business by aligning its practices to a
sustainable and inclusive future. Since 2009, Lenovo has
continued its role as a signatory supporter to the United
Nations Global Compact (UNGC), a globally recognized
platform that provides a blueprint for businesses that
want to achieve a more sustainable future for all. As a
business participant in the UNGC, the Company strives
to demonstrate continuous improvements by aligning
its operations and practices with the ten principles of
the UNGC. The principles promote a value system that
supports the fundamental responsibilities in the areas of
human rights, labor, environment and anti-corruption in
the markets where it operates.
As Lenovo aims to integrate these values and principles
wherever it conducts business, Lenovo is also making
meaningful contributions to people and the planet and
setting the stage for long-term success. This dedication
begins at the top with the support and endorsement
of its Chairman and CEO, Mr. Yang Yuanqing. Lenovo is
proud to be recognized by professionals and prominent
programs worldwide as it demonstrates leadership in
corporate ESG practices. The information below contains

Key ESG recognitions and accomplishments
Environmental
Lenovo scored in the leadership band by CDP for climate change (A) and water security (A) for 2025. This marks
the first time Lenovo has earned an A rating for both climate change and water security in the same year. For its
As, Lenovo also earned spots on CDP’s 2025 Corporate A list for Climate and Water which highlights Lenovo’s
commitment to environmental transparency and comprehensive disclosure.
Lenovo was named a SERI Champion of Electronics Sustainability, a designation that highlights the Company’s
industry-leading commitment to responsible electronics reuse, recycling, and circularity. The recognition reflects
Lenovo’s long-standing work to integrate the Responsible Recycling (R2) standard into its global Asset Recovery
Services, ensuring used electronics are managed with the highest levels of environmental and data security
compliance.
Multiple Lenovo initiatives were recipients at the Business Intelligence Group (BIG) Sustainability Awards in 2025.
Lenovo’s Solutions and Services Group (SSG) was honored with Sustainability Initiative of the Year for Lenovo
Intelligent Sustainability Solutions Advisor (LISSA) and Sustainability Service of Year for its Asset Recovery Service (ARS).

Social
Lenovo was recognized as a “2025 Best Place to Work for Disability Inclusion” by the Disability Equality Index
in the US, the UK and Brazil. The index is the world’s most comprehensive benchmarking tool for measuring
disability workplace inclusion. 2025 is the fifth year Lenovo was recognized in the US, and the second year of
recognition in the UK and Brazil.
For the second year in a row, Lenovo was recognized with ‘Ambassador’ status for 2025 in the Workplace Pride
Global Benchmark, reaffirming its position as a leader in LGBTIQ+ workplace inclusion. In addition to recognition
from Workplace Pride, Lenovo was included in the Human Rights Campaign Foundation’s 2026 Corporate
Equality Index for the eighth year, highlighting its commitment to workplace inclusion in the US.
In 2025, Lenovo was included in both Forbes World’s Top Companies for Women and Forbes World’s Best
Employers. Based on an independent global survey, these recognitions capture the voices of thousands of
employees across companies and industries, and are a validation of the inclusive culture Lenovo is continuously
working to grow and strengthen.
Governance
Lenovo was presented with a ‘Most Sustainable Organization’ award under the newly established Elite Past Winners (EPW)
section from the Hong Kong Institute of Certified Public Accountants (HKICPA). The EPW section was introduced
this year to celebrate companies with consistent outstanding performance and continued recognition from HKICPA (at
least five times over the past decade). This year’s award marks the thirteenth consecutive year that Lenovo has
been recognized by the HKICPA.
Lenovo was included in the 2025 Hang Seng Corporate Sustainability Index with an AA+ score. Within its industry,
Lenovo achieved the highest score in the environmental and social areas.
Lenovo was honored to be recognized in the world’s Top 100 Most Sustainable Companies, according to the 2026
Corporate Knights Global 100 ranking unveiled at the organization’s Annual CEO Roundtable during the World
Economic Forum in Davos.
Lenovo was awarded the Platinum Medal by EcoVadis, a leading global provider of business sustainability
ratings. With a score of 86/100, this recognition places Lenovo in the top 1% of all companies rated worldwide by
EcoVadis.

We are Lenovo
The “We Are Lenovo” cultural values of Serving Our
Customers, Innovation, Entrepreneurship, and Teamwork
with Integrity & Trust are the foundation of our business
strategy. These values guide our operations, decision-
making, and interactions with customers.
While “We Are Lenovo” continues to provide the
Company’s cultural foundation, Entrepreneurship 5.0 was
introduced to support an organization-wide mindset shift
for the AI era. It encourages employees at all levels to
align around Lenovo’s vision, lead innovation and change,
and act boldly in addressing emerging opportunities and
challenges. This evolution reflects Lenovo’s commitment
to strengthening organizational adaptability and long-
term competitiveness in a rapidly changing environment.
Employee Performance and Total Rewards
Lenovo continues to leverage its performance
management and total rewards programs to reinforce
a strong commitment to excellence in business
achievements and customer experience while providing
employees with an environment in which employees and
their families can thrive.
The performance management approach includes
annual goal setting and review, continuous feedback
for development, the calibration of individual ratings
across organizations to ensure a fair assessment, and the
recognition of individual and team performance while
driving business objectives and career development
which enables employees to grow and succeed through
challenging and exciting work.
Lenovo’s Total Rewards Philosophy integrates competitive,
performance-based compensation with a holistic benefits
and well-being approach, supported by a five-pillar
framework — Body, Mind, Prosperity, Connection, and
Purpose — reinforcing a commitment to supporting the
whole person.
Grounded in local market practices and delivered through
a consistent global framework, Total Rewards at Lenovo
reinforces equity, market relevance, and a clear connection
between performance, contribution, and reward outcomes.
Compensation programs are designed with flexibility to
support business-specific needs, while benefits and well-
being programs operate within a globally coordinated
operating model that promotes consistency, compliance,
cost sustainability, and disciplined decision-making —
allowing for local adaptation to address diverse employee
needs and market conditions.
Through the integrated design of performance,
compensation, benefits, and well-being, Lenovo seeks
to foster a healthy, resilient, and engaged workforce that
drives high performance, supports talent attraction and
retention, and delivers a consistent, positive employee
experience aligned with business priorities and evolving
workforce needs.
Organizational & Talent management
“Integrity, Learning Ability, Persistence and Ambition
are not only the fundamental for success in career
development, but also the compulsory talent requirement
in Lenovo. Only if you have these four elements you can
grow up into a big tree!” — Yuanqing Yang
Lenovo uses Tree model to vividly describe our talent
concept. The growing big tree symbolizes lofty ambitions;
the Leaves represent learning ability, continuously
acquiring new knowledge and mastering new skills; the
Trunk represents persistence, tenacity and perseverance;
the Root represents fundamental of human being —
integrity.
Lenovo’s long-term organization and talent strategy
remains steadily focused on hiring and engaging
world-class talent, building global organization and
talent capabilities and skills, and fostering an inclusive
environment where people can thrive at their full potential.
Training & Development
We have enhanced our management and leadership
development programs to provide support for managers
during their leadership progression at Lenovo by offering
specific training and development experiences, for
example Executive Presence Workshop (EPW), Leadership
Advancement for Transformation (LAT), Coaching With
Impact (CWI), Leading In Matrix Environment (LIME), etc
at key points in their careers.
Lenovo also places a high priority on executive leaders
development, bringing senior leaders together once a year
to share best practices, learn from external experts and
drive strategic alignment across the enterprise through
Global Leadership Team (GLT), Excelling Leadership In
Transformation Era (ELITE) and Striving Excellence in
Executive Director (SEED).
To accelerate our journey toward becoming an AI-
ready organization, we have developed and delivered a
comprehensive series of AI training sessions. These efforts
are building the capabilities our talent needs to adopt AI
responsibly across the business and to unlock productivity
improvements throughout the company.

For the year ended March 31, 2026, the Group achieved total sales of approximately US$83,075 million. When compared

mix and the lower profitability in ISG business; profit attributable to equity holders for the year increased by US$528
million to approximately US$1,912 million. Basic and diluted earnings per share were US15.63 cents and US13.91 cents,

Use of non-HKFRS measures
To supplement Lenovo’s consolidated financial statements prepared and presented in accordance with HKFRS
Accounting Standards (“HKFRS”), we utilize non-HKFRS adjusted profit as an additional financial measure.
We define adjusted profit as profit for the year by excluding (i) net fair value changes on financial assets at fair value
through profit or loss, (ii) amortization of intangible assets resulting from mergers and acquisitions, (iii) gain on
deemed disposal of a subsidiary, (iv) impairment and write-off of intangible assets, property, plant and equipment and
construction-in-progress, (v) fair value change on derivative financial liabilities relating to warrants, (vi) notional interest
on convertible bonds, (vii) dilution gain on interests in associates and a joint venture, (viii) impairment of interests in
associates, (ix) restructuring charges, and (x) one-time income tax credit; and the corresponding income tax effects, if
any.
More specifically, management excludes each of those items mentioned above for the following reasons:
(i) Lenovo recognizes fair value gains or losses from its strategic investments. The change in fair value included
revaluation gains or losses on new investment rounds on unlisted holdings and mark-to-market gains or losses
on listed holdings. Lenovo excludes this item for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate
a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating performance and comparisons to operating
performance in other periods.
(ii) Lenovo incurs charges related to the amortization of intangible assets resulting from mergers and acquisitions.
Those charges are included in Lenovo’s net profit prepared under HKFRS. Such charges are significantly impacted
by the timing and magnitude of Lenovo’s acquisitions and any related impairment charges. Consequently, Lenovo
excludes these charges for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful
evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(iii) Lenovo recognizes gain on deemed disposal of a subsidiary. Such gains or losses are inconsistent in amount and
frequency and are significantly impacted by the timing and nature of the transactions. Lenovo excludes this item
for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s
current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(iv) Lenovo records impairment and write-off of intangible assets, property, plant and equipment and construction-
in-progress, which are inconsistent in amount and frequency. Lenovo excludes these charges for the purposes
of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating
performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(v) Lenovo recognizes fair value change on derivative financial liabilities relating to warrants. Lenovo excludes this item
for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s
current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(vi) Lenovo incurs notional interest on convertible bonds, which is non-cash in nature. Lenovo excludes these charges
for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s
current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(vii) Lenovo recognizes dilution gain on interests in associates and a joint venture. Such gains or losses are inconsistent
in amount and frequency and are significantly impacted by the timing and nature of the transactions. Lenovo
excludes this item for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful
evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
==== page 49 ====
Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
47
(viii) Lenovo records impairment of interests in associates, which are inconsistent in amount and frequency. Lenovo
excludes these charges for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful
evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(ix) Lenovo incurs restructuring charges that are primarily costs associated with restructuring plans which are related to
employee separation from service and non-recurring costs for assets impairment. Lenovo excludes these charges
for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s
current operating performance and comparisons to operating performance in other periods.
(x) Lenovo recognizes one-time income tax credit, which is non-recurring in nature. Some organizational changes have
been made to support the Group’s future business planning and the growth of its global business, which resulted in
one-time income tax credit. Lenovo excludes this item for the purposes of calculating the non-HKFRS measure to
facilitate a more meaningful evaluation of Lenovo’s current operating performance and comparisons to operating
performance in other periods.
This non-HKFRS financial measure is not computed in accordance with, or as an alternative to, HKFRS. Management
uses this non-HKFRS financial measure for the purposes of evaluating Lenovo’s historical and prospective financial
performance. Management believes that excluding the items mentioned above for this non-HKFRS financial measure
allows management to better understand Lenovo’s consolidated financial performance in relation to its operating results,
as management does not believe that the excluded items are reflective of ongoing operating results.
However, the use of this particular non-HKFRS measure has limitations as an analytical tool, and should not be
considered in isolation from, or as a substitute for analysis of, the results of operations or financial conditions as reported
under HKFRS. In addition, this non-HKFRS financial measure may be defined differently from similar terms used by other
companies and therefore may not be comparable to similar measures used by other companies.
Reconciliations of the non-HKFRS financial measure to the most directly comparable HKFRS financial measure are
included in the tables below.

Property, plant and equipment
Property, plant and equipment comprise mainly the Group’s freehold land and buildings, leasehold improvements, plant
and machinery, furniture and fixtures, office equipment, equipment held for lease and motor vehicles. The 15 percent
increase is mainly attributable to the Group’s investments in equipment held for lease, office equipment and plant and
machinery, transfer of completed assets from construction-in-progress, to cope with business growth, and exchange
adjustments; partly offset by current year depreciation.
Right-of-use assets
Right-of-use assets comprise mainly the land use rights in respect of the manufacturing sites and headquarters in the
Mainland of China (“Chinese Mainland”), and leases of land and buildings for manufacturing sites and offices in Chinese
Mainland and overseas. The 10 percent increase is mainly attributable to lease renewals and new leases entered into
during the year, partly offset by current year depreciation and lease contracts terminated during the year.
Construction-in-progress
Construction-in-progress comprise mainly the Group’s investments in manufacturing sites and office buildings, internal
use software and patent and technology under development. Internal use software under development mainly
comprises online platform development and system enhancement for business operations. The 93 percent increase is
mainly attributable to further investment in internal use software, technology and buildings under construction during
the year, partly offset by transfer of completed assets to property, plant and equipment and intangible assets.
Intangible assets
Intangible assets comprise goodwill and other intangible assets including trademarks and trade names, customer
relationships, patents and technology, internal use software and exclusive rights. The 4 percent decrease is mainly due to
current year amortization, impairment and write-off of patents and technology, partly offset by additional investments in
patents and technology and transfer of completed internal use software from construction-in-progress to cope with the
growth of business and exchange adjustments.
The Group completed the impairment test for goodwill allocated to the Group’s various cash generating units (“CGUs”)
by comparing their recoverable amounts to their carrying amounts as at the reporting date. The recoverable amount
of a CGU is determined based on value in use. These assessments use pre-tax cash flow projections based on financial
budgets approved by management covering a five-year period with a terminal value related to the future cash flow of
the CGU extrapolated using constant projection of cash flows beyond the five-year period. The directors are of the view
that there was no indication of impairment of goodwill based on impairment tests performed.
Interests in associates and joint ventures
Interests in associates and joint ventures comprise the share of net assets of and loan to associates and joint ventures.
The 15 percent decrease is mainly attributable to the impairment of associates and share of losses, partly offset by
additional investments and exchange adjustments during the year.
Deferred income tax assets
Deferred income tax assets amounted to US$3,762 million as at year end, representing an increase of 23 percent, which
is mainly attributable to temporary differences in relation to tax depreciation allowance, provisions and accruals and
deferred revenue arising in the normal course of business, partly offset by utilization and reversal of tax losses. Deferred
income tax assets are recognized to the extent that realization of the related tax benefit through the future taxable
profits is probable.
Financial assets at fair value through profit or loss
Financial assets at fair value through profit or loss increased by 39 percent during the year, which is mainly attributable
to additional investments, net fair value gain recognized and exchange adjustments, partly offset by disposal of certain
financial assets.

Financial assets at fair value through other comprehensive income increased by 9 percent during the year, which is
mainly attributable to net fair value gain recognized and exchange adjustments.

Inventories
The Group’s inventories comprise raw materials and work-in-progress, finished goods and service parts where raw
materials and work-in-progress accounted for more than 60 percent of total inventories. The Group’s inventories
purchase and production plan are primarily based on expectations on market demand. The 48 percent increase is mainly
attributable to the higher raw materials inventory level in response to the industry-wide component shortage which
has driven up component cost, and the Group strives to source more components to cope with the increase in market
demand.
Trade, lease and notes receivables
Trade, lease and notes receivables increased by 38 percent which is attributable to the increase in sales in the fourth
quarter of current year over the corresponding period of last year. The Group has a credit policy in place and exposures
to these credit risks are monitored on an ongoing basis. Majority of trade receivables are aged within 30 days based on
invoice date.
Derivative financial assets
Derivatives relate to foreign currency forward contracts that are designated as hedges for the fair value of recognized
assets or liabilities or a firm commitment, or of highly probable forecast transactions. Derivatives are initially recognized
at fair value on the date a derivative contract is entered into and are subsequently re-measured at their fair values. The
226 percent increase is mainly attributable to the fair value gain recognized for the derivatives during the year.
Deposits, prepayments and other receivables
Deposits, prepayments and other receivables mainly comprise amounts due from subcontractors for components
delivered in the ordinary course of business and other tax receivables. The 76 percent increase is mainly attributable
to the increase in amounts due from subcontractors as a result of more business activities during the fourth quarter of
current year than in the corresponding period of last year.

Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
51
Cash and cash equivalents
The 3 percent increase is mainly attributable to net cash generated from operating activities, partly offset by net cash
used in investing activities and financing activities. The working capital management enables the Group to maintain
sufficient cash to meet operational, financing and investing needs.

Borrowings
Borrowings (classified as non-current) decreased by US$475 million, mainly due to the reclassification of the 2029
Convertible Bonds from non-current liabilities to current liabilities, as the bondholders have the right to require the
Company to redeem the 2029 Convertible Bonds on a specific date within the next twelve months, partly offset by
notional interest of other convertible bonds and notes.
Warranty provision
The Group records warranty liabilities at the time of sale for the estimated costs that will be incurred under its basic
limited warranty. The specific warranty terms and conditions vary depending upon the product and the country in which
it was sold, but generally includes technical support, repair parts and labour associated with warranty repair and service
actions. The period ranges from one to three years. The Group revalues its estimates on a quarterly basis to assess the
adequacy of its recorded warranty liabilities and adjusts the amounts as necessary.

52
Deferred revenue
Deferred revenue (classified as non-current) primarily relates to the Group’s unfulfilled performance obligations over
extended warranty services at the reporting date for which consideration has been received. The 21 percent increase is
mainly due to the growth of business during the year.
Retirement benefit obligations
The Group operates various pension schemes. The schemes are generally funded through payments to insurance
companies or trustee-administered funds, determined by periodic actuarial calculations. The Group has both defined
benefit and defined contribution plans.
Deferred income tax liabilities
Deferred income tax liabilities amounted to US$311 million as at year end, representing an increase of 15 percent, which is
mainly attributable to the withholding tax on undistributed earnings and accelerated tax depreciation.
Derivative financial liabilities
Derivative financial liabilities (classified as non-current) represent the warrants issued by the Company. Derivatives are
initially recognized at fair value on the date that the warrant contract is entered into and are subsequently re-measured
at fair values. The 81 percent decrease is attributable to the net fair value gain recognized for the warrants during the
year and reclassification of a portion to current liabilities.
Other non-current liabilities
Other non-current liabilities mainly comprise liabilities arising from long-term lease liabilities, deferred consideration and
government incentives and grants received in advance. The 7 percent increase is mainly attributable to the new lease
contracts entered during the year.

Trade and notes payables
The increase in trade and notes payables by 61 percent is mainly attributable to the increase in business activities in the
fourth quarter of current year over the corresponding period of last year.
Derivative financial liabilities
Derivative financial liabilities (classified as current) relate to warrants issued by the Company and foreign currency
forward contracts that are designated as hedges for the fair value of recognized assets or liabilities or a firm
commitment, or of highly probable forecast transactions. Derivatives are initially recognized at fair value on the date a
derivative contract is entered into and are subsequently re-measured at fair values. The 50 percent decrease is mainly
attributable to the net fair value gain recognized for the foreign currency forward contracts and warrants during the year,
partly offset by reclassification of a portion of warrants to current liabilities.
==== page 55 ====
Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
53
Other payables and accruals
Other payables and accruals mainly comprise the obligations to pay for finished goods and services that have been
acquired in the ordinary course of business from subcontractors; allowance for billing adjustments relating primarily
to allowance for future volume discounts, price protection, rebates, and customer sales returns; accruals for salaries,
commission and bonus, written put option liabilities and lease liabilities. The increase of 24 percent is mainly attributable
to the increase in payables to subcontractors, allowance for billing adjustments and accruals for salaries, commission and
bonus driven by expanded business activities.
Provisions
Provisions comprise warranty provision (due within one year), environmental restoration and restructuring provisions.
The 11 percent increase is driven by the net increase in warranty provision driven by increase in business activities and the
restructuring provision made during the year.
Deferred revenue
Deferred revenue (classified as current) primarily relates to the Group’s unfulfilled performance obligations over
extended warranty services at the reporting date for which consideration has been received. The 27 percent increase is
mainly due to the growth of business during the year.
Income tax payable
Income tax payable doubled during the year, mainly driven by the Group’s higher assessable profit and change in
profitability of the Group’s subsidiaries in respective countries they are operating.
Borrowings
Borrowings (classified as current) decreased by US$152 million which is mainly attributable to the repayment of the
2025 Note upon maturity, partly offset by the reclassification of the 2029 Convertible Bonds from non-current liabilities
to current liabilities, as the bondholders have the right to require the Company to redeem the 2029 Convertible Bonds
on a specific date within the next twelve months.
Capital Expenditure
The Group incurred capital expenditure of US$1,859 million (2025: US$1,151 million) during the year ended March 31,
2026, mainly for the acquisition of property, plant and equipment, additions to construction-in-progress and intangible
assets. The higher capital expenditure incurred in current year is mainly attributable to more investments in patent and
technology, assets under construction and equipment held for lease.
Contingent Liabilities
The Group, in the ordinary course of its business, is involved in various claims, suits, investigations, and legal proceedings
that arise from time to time. Although the Group does not expect that the outcome in any of these legal proceedings,
individually or collectively, will have a material adverse effect on its financial position or results of operations, litigation
is inherently unpredictable. Therefore, the Group could incur judgments or enter into settlements of claims that could
adversely affect its operating results or cash flows in a particular period.
Liquidity and Financial Resources
At March 31, 2026, total assets of the Group amounted to US$57,129 million (2025: US$44,231 million), which were
financed by equity attributable to owners of the Company of US$7,635 million (2025: US$6,069 million), other non-
controlling interests (net of put option written on non-controlling interests) of US$809 million (2025: US$591 million),
and total liabilities of US$48,685 million (2025: US$37,571 million). At March 31, 2026, the current ratio of the Group was 0.95
(2025: 0.93).

==== page 59 ====
From the racetrack to the Media &
Technology Centre in Biggin Hill, UK,
Lenovo provides the AI devices, innovative
solutions, and robust IT infrastructure that
support everyone, from executives to track-
side operational engineers.
Every race weekend, Formula 1 is
responsible for securely collecting and
distributing enormous volumes of data,
including real-time timing, car telemetry
and tire data, as well as team radios and
all camera and audio feeds. This data is
shared with racing teams and the FIA
and transformed into thrilling content
delivered to more than 180 territories in
just seconds. In total, over 650 terabytes of
data are transferred each event weekend,
contributing to over 500 hours of live TV
broadcast throughout the season and more
than 200 hours of post-produced content.
The vast amount of data generated during
each event, combined with the globally
mobile nature of the sport – spanning
24 cities on five continents over just
nine months – means that F1 requires
exceptionally high levels of performance,
mobility, and reliability.  Lenovo’s
technology infrastructure is built to
meet these needs, powering split-second
decisions and keeping pace with the
breakneck innovation of motorsport.
Lenovo technology also supports F1’s
ambitious sustainability goals through
hardware consolidation enabled by
increased performance and processing
capabilities, as well as a strategy for end-
of-life tech that prioritizes sustainability
through Lenovo’s Asset Recovery Service.
F1 has also deployed Neptune® Liquid
Cooling technology, which enhances HPC
capabilities for more powerful AI and
data workloads while increasing energy
efficiency compared to traditional air-
cooled systems.
Formula 1® relies on Lenovo to power the
technological backbone that brings the
exhilaration of race day to more than
820 million fans all over the world.

Corporate
governance report
The Board also appointed Mr. John Lawson Thornton as
the lead independent director (the “Lead Independent
Director”) with broad authorities and responsibilities. Such
authorities and responsibilities include serving as chairman
of the Nomination and Governance Committee meeting
and/or the Board meeting considering the combined roles
of Chairman and CEO; in consultation with all other Board
members, to prepare an assessment of the performance
of the Chairman and/or CEO; calls and chair meeting(s)
with all non-executive directors at least once a year on
matters deemed appropriate and provide feedback
to the Chairman and/or CEO; and serves a key role in
the Board evaluation process. Accordingly, the Board
believes that the current Board structure with combined
roles of Chairman and CEO, the appointment of Lead
Independent Director and a majority of independent non-
executive directors provide an effective check and balance
of powers and authorizations between the Board and the
management of the Company.
In relation to the recommended best practices in the CG
Code, the Company has, where appropriate, adopted such
practices to enhance its corporate governance framework.
Notable practices include the publication of quarterly
financial results and business reviews, in addition to interim
and annual results, which enhance shareholders’ ability
to assess the Group’s performance, financial position and
prospects.  The quarterly financial results are prepared
using accounting standards consistent with those applied
to the interim and annual financial statements.
Corporate governance
principles and structure
The board of directors (the “Board”) and the management
of Lenovo Group Limited (the “Company”, together with
its subsidiaries, “Lenovo”, “Lenovo Group” or the “Group”)
strive to attain and uphold a high standard of corporate
governance and to maintain sound and well-established
corporate governance practices for the interest of
shareholders and other stakeholders including, but not
limited to, employees, customers, suppliers and the
general public. The Group abides strictly by the governing
laws and regulations of the jurisdictions where it operates
and observes the applicable guidelines and rules issued
by regulatory authorities. It regularly undertakes review of
its corporate governance system to ensure it is in line with
international and local best practices.
Compliance with Corporate Governance Code
Throughout the financial year ended March 31, 2026
(“FY2025/26”), the Company has complied with the code
provisions of the Corporate Governance Code (the “CG
Code”) as set out in Appendix C1 to the Rules Governing
the Listing of Securities on The Stock Exchange of Hong
Kong Limited (“Listing Rules”), and where appropriate,
met the recommended best practices in the CG Code,
with the exception that the roles of the chairman of the
Board (the “Chairman”) and the chief executive officer of
the Company (the “CEO”) have not been segregated as
required by code provision C.2.1 of the CG Code.
The Board has reviewed the Group’s organization human
resources planning and considers that combining the
roles of the Chairman and the CEO by Mr. Yang Yuanqing
(“Mr. Yang”) is appropriate and beneficial to the Group
as it provides consistency of the strategy execution
and stability in the operations of the Group. The Board,
comprising a majority of independent non-executive
directors, meets regularly on a quarterly basis to review
the operations of the Group led by Mr. Yang.

Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
61
Corporate culture
“We Are Lenovo” represents the Company’s established culture and the way employees work together as one team.
Its core values-Serving our Customers, Entrepreneurship, Innovation, and Teamwork with Integrity & Trust-provide the
foundation that guides employee behavior and supports the execution of the Company’s vision, mission, and strategy.
In response to the evolving external environment, including the rapid development of artificial intelligence technologies,
Lenovo has introduced Entrepreneurship 5.0 as an extension of its existing cultural framework, to clarify expected
behaviors and leadership practices in the current business context.
Entrepreneurship 5.0 focuses on three dimensions, which are also illustrated in the diagram below:
•	 Commit	to	Lenovo	Vision
•	 Innovate	and	Lead	Change
•	 Act	Boldly
Entrepreneurship 5.0 was formally introduced by the Chairman/CEO at the FY2025/26 Kick-off Meeting and
subsequently formally launched at the Global Leadership Team (GLT) meeting in September 2025 as a key cultural
focus for the AI era. Since its launch, the Company has observed increasing adoption of these principles across the
organization, with employees and leaders beginning to apply them in daily work and decision-making.
==== page 64 ====
Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
62
Corporate governance report
The Board recognises that Lenovo’s culture is integral to the execution of its strategy and the achievement of its
vision and mission. The “We Are Lenovo” culture, reinforced by Entrepreneurship 5.0, guides leadership behaviours
and decision making, supporting innovation, accountability and agility in advancing the 3S strategy and intelligent
transformation. The Board oversees the alignment of culture with the Company’s purpose, vision, mission and strategic
priorities.
The Board is responsible for ensuring that the Company’s culture remains aligned with its vision, mission, values and
strategy. The Board meets at least quarterly, during which the CEO and the Chief Financial Officer submit reports,
together with updates on key strategic matters, for the Board’s consideration. These reports enable the Board to monitor
and evaluate the implementation of the Group’s strategy and its performance. Relevant information on the Company’s
culture and 3S Strategy is disclosed in the “Chairman & CEO Statement” and “Management’s discussion & analysis”
sections of this annual report.

Corporate governance report
Key features of the Board
Balanced composition and diversity
The Board maintains an appropriate balance of gender, ethnicity, cultural background, skills and
experience,  with a mix of regional and industry expertise to support effective deliberation and
decision-making.
Strong independent oversight
The current composition of the Board exceeds the requirements under Rules 3.10 and 3.10A of the
Listing Rules, as a majority of its members are independent non-executive directors, with the ability to
exercise independent judgement.
An independent non-executive director of the Company, Mr. John Lawson Thornton, has been
appointed as the Lead Independent Director to further strengthen Board oversight and governance.
This is consistent with the CG Code requirements where the Board does not have an independent
chair. The roles and responsibilities of the Lead Independent Director are set out on page 63 of this
annual report.
Robust Board skills matrix
The Board is supported by a skills matrix, ensuring a balanced mix of competencies aligned with the
Group’s strategic priorities, global operations and evolving business environment. Details of the Board
skills matrix are set out in page 69 of this annual report.

The Nomination and Governance Committee regularly reviews the Board’s composition,
independence and succession planning to maintain its effectiveness.
Regular Board evaluation
The Board conducts biennial evaluations of its performance and effectiveness to support continuous
improvement. Details of the Board evaluation are set out on pages 96 and 97 of this annual report.
Oversight of corporate culture and ESG matters
The Board oversees corporate culture and ESG matters, ensuring alignment with the Group’s strategy
and providing strategic oversight of ESG priorities, risks and performance.

Board diversity policy
The Board values diversity as a factor in selecting candidates to serve on the Board and believes that the diversity which
exists in its composition provides significant benefits to the Board and the Company and forms an important part of the
nomination policy (the “Nomination Policy”) as adopted by the Board.
The Board believes that a key success factor of an effective Board is that it comprises a range and balance of skills,
experience, knowledge and independence, with individuals that work as a team. The adoption of Board Diversity Policy
is to ensure that diversity in its broadest sense continues to remain a feature of the Board. All Board appointments are
made on merit, in the context of the skills, experience and gender diversity, the Board as a whole requires being effective.
The details of the appointment process are disclosed on page 70 of this annual report.

Workforce inclusion
As a global technology leader, Lenovo’s workplace inclusion programs and initiatives drive our business forward.
Lenovo understands that inclusion is vital for innovation and seeks to create a workplace where everyone feels valued,
respected, and can reach their full potential. Lenovo has offered programs and opportunities to promote understanding
and inclusion across its global workforce for nearly 20 years. These include special trainings designed to help individuals
develop across every phase of their career, from individual contributor to management, as well as guidance for bias
mitigation with hiring managers. After meeting and exceeding 2020 executive representation goals, Lenovo continues
to advance inclusion from top executive ranks to product development teams and early career hiring efforts. Currently
with 25% executive representation of women, an industry-leading 29% representation of women on technical teams, and
37% representation of women in its global workforce, Lenovo is persistently working towards fair and inclusive workforce
recruitment and advancement.
Lenovo also fosters an array of employee resource groups around the world, promoting understanding and inclusion
in the workforce across all identities including gender, cultural background and ability. Lenovo’s inclusive culture has
extended to its products through the Inclusive Product Design Office (IPDO), which seeks to integrate feedback from
all consumer segments early in product development. As of March 31, 2026, the IPDO has met its goal to review 75%
of Lenovo’s products to ensure they are inclusive, no matter a user’s background or ability. Lenovo is a signatory of the
United Nations Women Empowerment Principles and has been recognized as a Best Place to Work for people with
disabilities in the United States, Brazil, and the United Kingdom in 2025. With employees in more than 60 countries
serving customers across 180 markets, Lenovo is reliant on the principles of mutual respect and inclusion and the unique
strength these cultural values provide to its global business.

Corporate governance report
Tenure
In accordance with the articles of association of the Company (the “Articles of Association”), all directors are subject to
retirement by rotation at least once every three years. At each annual general meeting, one-third of the directors then in
office shall retire from office and shall be eligible for re-election. Any new appointments, whether to fill a casual vacancy
or as an addition to the Board, are subject to re-election by shareholders of the Company at the next following annual
general meeting of the Company. The chart below shows the tenure of the Board members as of the date of this annual
report.
All non-executive directors (including independent non-executive directors) have entered into letters of appointment
with the Company for a term of three years. Their appointment are subject to retirement by rotation at least once every
three years and re-election at the annual general meeting in accordance with the Articles of Association.
The Company recognises that the independence of directors is an important principle of good corporate governance.
The Board adopted the principle (the “Principle”) that each term of an independent non-executive director of the
Company shall not exceed three years and is subject to re-election by shareholders at any subsequent annual general
meeting of the Company. Such term may be renewable for additional three-year periods, up to a maximum of nine
years. This Principle reflects the Company’s established governance practices and is aligned with prevailing corporate
governance best practices, including those stipulated in the CG Code. Upon the recommendation of the Nomination and
Governance Committee and subject to compliance with the applicable requirements under the CG Code, the Board may,
on a case-by-case basis,  invite an independent non-executive director to serve for an additional three-year term, up
to a total of 12 years subject to re-election at any subsequent annual general meeting of the Company. Such extension
arrangement will cease to apply following the 2031 annual general meeting of the Company, with a view to achieving full
alignment with the prevailing CG Code requirements relating to directors’ tenure.
In accordance with the Articles of Association, Mr. Zhao John Huan, Mr. John Lawson Thornton, Mr. Gordon Robert
Halyburton Orr and Ms. Cher Wang Hsiueh Hong will retire at the forthcoming annual general meeting to be held on July
23, 2026 (“2026 Annual General Meeting”), and being eligible, will offer themselves for re-election at the 2026 Annual
General Meeting.

of effective long-term strategies. The diagram below illustrates how the Board has been refreshed since May 2022.

Nomination, appointment and election
Nomination policy
The Nomination Policy guides the Nomination and Governance Committee and the Board on nomination of candidates
for the Board. This policy sets out the selection criteria, tenure, election/re-election requirements and nomination
procedure, details of which are set out below.
Nomination and appointment process
The Board recognises the need to ensure the Board and senior management are always well resourced, with the suitable
individuals possessing the requisite skills and experience to deliver the Group’s strategy.
There is a formal and transparent procedure for the appointment of new directors to the Board, the primary
responsibility of which has been delegated to the Nomination and Governance Committee. The Nomination and
Governance Committee is chaired by an independent non-executive director and composed of the Chairman (also the
executive director), one non-executive director and two other independent non-executive directors. This composition
supports objective and independent decision-making in the best interest of the Company.
The Nomination and Governance Committee’s assessment of the candidates includes, but not limited to, the
independence under the Listing Rules, consideration of the relevant knowledge and diversity of backgrounds, gender,
skills, experience and perspectives that would complement the existing Board.
The nomination process involves a structured evaluation and selection process, including the key stages set out below.
The Nomination and Governance Committee makes recommendations to the Board, which retains ultimate responsibility
for the appointment of directors.

Independence
The independent non-executive directors do not participate in the day-to-day management of the Group and do
not engage in any business dealing or other relationships with the Group (other than in situations permitted by the
applicable regulations), in order to ensure that they can exercise independent judgement and act in the best interests of
the Company and its shareholders.
Each independent non-executive director has provided a confirmation of independence to the Company. On May 20,
2026, the Nomination and Governance Committee conducted an annual review of the independence of all independent
non-executive directors of the Company for FY2025/26. Having taken into account the factors as set out in rule 3.13
of the Listing Rules in assessing the independence of independent non-executive directors, the Nomination and
Governance Committee (with the relevant committee member abstaining from voting on the resolutions concerning his/
her own independence) concluded that all independent non-executive directors satisfied the criteria of independence
as set out in the Listing Rules. Based on the Nomination and Governance Committee’s review and the independence
assessment process as set out on page 72, the Company confirmed that all the independent non-executive directors are
independent.
The Nomination and Governance Committee affirmed that all independent non-executive directors of the Company
constitute a strong independent element on the Board, are free from any business or other relationship which could
materially interfere with the exercise of their judgement, and remained independent for FY2025/26 and as of the date of
this annual report. The diagram below shows the independence weighting of the Board and the Board Committees as of
the date of this annual report.

Independence assessment
Before and on appointment
•	 The	Nomination	and	Governance	Committee	will	evaluate	the	suitability	of	the	candidates,	including	an
assessment of their independence, skills and experience, and, where appropriate,  nominate them for the
Board’s consideration and approval.
•	 A	proposed	new	independent	non-executive	director	is	required	to	confirm	with	the	Company	his/her
independence pursuant to rule 3.13 of the Listing Rules.
Ongoing process
•	 Each	independent	non-executive	director	is	required	to	inform	the	Company	and	the	Stock	Exchange,	as
soon as practicable, of any subsequent change of circumstances that may affect his/her independence.
•	 The	independent	non-executive	directors	are	required	to	confirm	to	the	Company,	on	a	semi-annual	basis,
whether they have any financial, business, family or other material/relevant relationship with each other.
•	 All	directors	have	a	continuing	obligation	to	update	the	Company	on	any	changes	to	their	other
appointments, which will be reviewed by the Company.
Annual assessment
•	 Each	independent	non-executive	director	is	requested	to	confirm	to	the	Company	his/her	independence
with reference to the criteria under rule 3.13 of the Listing Rules.
•	 The	Nomination	and	Governance	Committee	assesses	and	reviews	the	independence	of	independent	non-
executive directors on an annual basis.
Mechanisms for ensuring independent views and input
The Company has established mechanism(s) to ensure that independent views and input are available to the Board.
Having reviewed the implementation and effectiveness, and taking into account the following channels, it was considered
that these mechanisms remain effective to ensure a strong independent element on the Board:
•	 The	Board	comprises	a	majority	of	independent	non-executive	directors,	representing	58%	of	the	Board	as	at	the
date of this report.
•	 A	Lead	Independent	Director	has	been	appointed	with	broad	authorities	and	responsibilities.	Among	others,	the
Lead Independent Director convenes and chairs meetings with all non-executive directors without management
and executive director present at least once a year. During FY2025/26, the Lead Independent Director and
chairmen of the Board Committees met all other non-executive directors, without management, at executive
sessions held after the respective board meetings and committee meetings, where applicable.
•	 All	independent	non-executive	directors	share	their	views	and	opinions	through	regular	quarterly	meetings	where
heads of core divisions and particular business units would be invited to attend such meetings and present business
updates.
•	 Independent	non-executive	directors	are	authorised	to	seek	independent	professional	advice	at	the	Company’s
expense, where necessary, to perform their responsibilities.
•	 The	Chairman	has	one-to-one	meetings	with	each	director,	who	may	share	their	views	directly	with	the	Chairman.

Conflicts of interest
Directors have a statutory duty to avoid situations in which
they have, or may have, interests that conflict with those
of the Company. The Board has established procedures
and guidelines to address actual or potential conflicts of
interests of directors, as follows:
•	 The	Board	considers	each	appointment	on	its
individual merit and takes into account all relevant
circumstances.
•	 Before	 taking	on	additional	responsibilities	or	external
appointments, directors are obliged to ensure that
they can meet the expected time commitment for
their role at the Company and that no potential
conflicts arise from positions held with another
companies.
•	 Transactions	involving	directors	or	their	related	parties
are handled by other directors. For example, matters
regarding remuneration of the executive director are
handled by the Compensation Committee.
•	 Under	the	Articles	of	Association,	directors	are	also
required to declare any direct or indirect interests, in
any proposal, transaction, arrangement or contract
that is significant in relation to the Company’s
business and the director’s interest or his/her
associate’s interest or the interest of the entity
connected with the director is material.
All potential conflicts of interest will be recorded and
reviewed annually by the Nomination and Governance
Committee to ensure that the procedures operate
effectively.

The Board considers that all directors devote sufficient
time and attention to the affairs of the Company. Directors
are provided with guidelines on time commitments, and
appropriate confirmations are provided through their
letters of appointment and annual disclosure letters.

and nature of offices held in Hong Kong and/or overseas
listed public companies or organisations and other
significant commitments, including their positions and
an indication of time involved. They are required to notify
the Company in a timely manner of any changes and

Directors’ securities transactions
The Company has adopted the Model Code for Securities Transactions by Directors of Listed Issuers (the “Model Code”)
set out in Appendix C3 to the Listing Rules. Based on the principles of the Model Code, the Company has established a
comprehensive and operative company policy governing directors’ securities transactions. All directors of the Company
have confirmed, following specific enquiry, that they complied with the required standards during FY2025/26.
The Company has also adopted a securities trading policy applicable to designated senior management of the Group,
on terms no less stringent than the required standards as set out in the Model Code.

Continuous professional development program
Lenovo believes that effective leadership is supported by continuous learning. The Company provides directors and
senior executives with a structured and comprehensive continuous professional development program annually to
ensure that they maintain and enhance the skills and knowledge required to discharge their duties effectively.
The program is designed to deepen participants’ understanding of the Group’s business and industry developments,
strengthen governance and oversight capabilities, and keep directors and senior executives informed of emerging trends
and regulatory developments. The program includes, but is not limited to, the following:
•	 engaging	presentations	by	senior	executives	and	external	experts	on	the	latest	trends	in	technology,	geopolitics
and, corporate strategy, and updates on ESG, corporate governance, risk management and internal controls, and
key legal and regulatory developments;
•	 participation	in	global	internal	and	external	events	in	the	industry	to	stay	abreast	of	industry	trends,	regulatory
developments, and emerging technologies;
•	 interactive	site	visits	across	Lenovo’s	global	campuses	and	factories,	giving	directors	first-hand	insight	into	the
Group’s operations and supply chain; and
•	 focused	discussions	on	critical	emerging	topics	to	support	strategic	thinking.
During the year, our directors and senior executives were invited to attend professional development activities as follows:
Events

To enhance directors’ understanding of Lenovo’s culture and its alignment with the Group’s values, vision and
strategy, directors were invited to attend the Global Leadership Team (GLT) Meeting held on September 23-25,

enablement, leadership and networking, and the cultural framework Entrepreneurship 5.0. Discussions addressed

business and long-term strategy.

Lenovo Group Limited  2025/26 Annual Report
80
Corporate governance report
Industry congress
Lenovo operates in technology industry characterised by rapidly evolving market trends, changing consumer
preferences, and continuous technological innovation. To ensure that directors remain well informed of the latest
industry developments, emerging technologies and product advancements, the Company has implemented
initiatives to provide timely exposure to relevant technological and market insights. Directors were invited to
attend Consumer Electronic Show (CES), held together with Lenovo Tech World in Las Vegas in January 2026
and Mobile World Congress (MWC) in Barcelona in March 2026. These events provided the best product reviews,
demonstrations and displays that showcase the technologies leveraging the power of AI both from the Group and
other market players.
These events also facilitated the stakeholders’ understanding, including that of directors and customers, of how
Lenovo embraces the business opportunities in this dynamic market and satisfies the customer needs. Key topics
covered Hybrid AI Defines Our Era, Personal AI, Enterprise AI and AI for Good.
Expert briefings and seminars
The Company arranges in-house expert briefings and seminars on a regular basis to keep directors informed of
matters relating to the Group’s business, strategy and operating environment. Directors are also encouraged to
attend relevant external professional programs, at the Company’s expense where appropriate, to remain abreast
of developments in the broader business and regulatory landscape in which the Group operates.
During FY2025/26, directors participated in a range of internal and external training activities, including in-house
and external expert briefings and seminars featuring in-depth discussions on topics such as AI, geopolitical
developments and tariff impacts, corporate strategy updates and regulatory developments. Anti-bribery
and corruption training was also provided during the year to update directors on relevant global regulatory
requirements applicable to the Group.
Through participation in key strategic engagements, including leadership forums, global technology showcases,
strategic partnerships, site visits and industry congresses, directors gained meaningful exposure to Lenovo’s
technology capabilities, smart manufacturing practices and innovation ecosystem. These hands-on experiences
provided insights into industry trends, partner perspectives and operational excellence, enhancing directors’
understanding of how innovation and disciplined execution support the Group’s strategic objectives, long-term
vision and sustainable growth.
Regulatory updates
Directors are kept informed, on a continuing basis, of regulatory developments by the Company Secretary,
including the introduction of new regulations and guidelines and amendments issued by the Stock Exchange and
other relevant regulatory authorities, together with their implications for the Company, the Group and directors’
duties.
In addition, the directors’ induction handbook, which sets out the Group’s organisational structure, Board policies,
corporate rules and policies, and other relevant legal and regulatory reference materials, is updated from time to
time to reflect the latest developments.

Training Coverage and CPD Summary for FY2025/26
Directors are required to undertake continuous professional development covering the five specified topics pursuant to rule 3.09G
of the Listing Rules as set out below. The Company maintains records of directors’ training for review by the Nomination
and Governance Committee, supporting appropriate and transparent disclosure of directors’ training activities.
The Nomination and Governance Committee will, on a continuing basis, evaluate and determine the training and
development needs of the directors, particularly on the following five specified topics (the “Five Specified Topics”):
(a) the roles, functions and responsibilities of the Board, the Board Committees and directors, and board effectiveness;
(b) the Company’s obligations and directors’ duties under Hong Kong law and the Listing Rules, and key legal and
regulatory developments, including Listing Rule updates, relevant to the discharge of such obligations and duties;
(c) corporate governance and ESG matters, including developments in sustainability or climate-related risks and
opportunities relevant to the Company and its business;
(d) risk management and internal controls; and
(e) updates on industry-specific developments, business trends and strategies relevant to the Company.
Directors have provided the Company with records of the training undertaken during FY2025/26 covering the Five
Specified Topics, including internal training through Board and its committee meetings, external professional training
where applicable, and self-study. The relevant details are set out below:

extent permitted by applicable laws and regulations.
The Company has maintained appropriate directors’ and officers’ liability insurance throughout the year, which provides
cover for certain legal actions brought against its directors and officers.

responsible for facilitating the Board process, and communications among Board members, and between the Board,
shareholders and management. During FY2025/26, Ms. Lam undertook appropriate professional training to update her
skills and knowledge.

Corporate governance report
Board Effectiveness
Board’s responsibilities and delegation to
management
The Company is led by the Board, which is responsible
for steering the Group’s success by overseeing its overall
strategy and directing and supervising its affairs in a
responsible and effective manner. The Board also sets the
Group’s core values and adopts appropriate standards
to ensure that the Group operates with integrity and
complies with the relevant rules and regulations.
The Board retains overall responsibility for the Group’s
leadership, strategy and governance, including oversight
of risk management and internal control systems. The
Board also ensures that appropriate arrangements are in
place for effective oversight of succession planning and
remuneration through the relevant Board Committees.
The Company has established a clear framework for
the division of responsibilities between the Board and
management. A formal schedule of matters is reserved
for the Board, while other matters are delegated to the
management.
The Board retains responsibility for key matters, including
overseeing the management of the Company and
its business. Key matters reserved for the Board are
summarized in the table below:
The Board has given clear directions to management
as to the matters that must be approved by the Board
before decisions are made on behalf of the Company or
commitments are entered into on behalf of the Group.
The Board delegates the day-to-day management,
administration and operation of the Group to management
under the leadership of the CEO, including:
•	 implementation	of	strategies	and	direction
determined by the Board;
•	 operation	of	the	Group’s	businesses;
•	 preparation	of	financial	statements	and	operating
budgets; and
•	 compliance	with	applicable	laws	and	regulations.
These arrangements will be reviewed periodically to
ensure that they remain appropriate to the Group’s needs.
3

Corporate governance report
Other key features of Board process
Timely updates
and discussion
The directors are provided with all relevant meeting documentation and
financial information in a timely manner to assist them in discharging their
duties. Monthly updates on the Group’s financial performance are provided to
the Board between regular Board Meetings.
In addition to standing agenda items, “deep-dive” topics are included and
discussed, where applicable. During FY2025/26, “deep-dive” presentations
included, among others, the Group’s specific strategy updates, special projects
and changes in Board compositions.
In addition to regular quarterly Board (earnings) meetings, board meetings
focusing on the Group’s strategy would also be held before or after the regular
Board (earnings) meetings whenever necessary.
Senior management are invited to attend Board meetings, where appropriate,
to report on matters relating to their areas of responsibility; to brief the
directors on recommendations submitted for the Board’s consideration; and
to furnish additional information or explanation, particularly for complex and
technical matters tabled to the Board.
Executive sessions As a good corporate governance practice, separate executive sessions were
arranged for: (i) the Chairman to meet with independent non-executive
directors in the absence of management; and (ii) the Lead Independent
Director to meet with other non-executive directors in the absence of the
executive director and management, to discuss any issue or matters they
would like to raise.
Professional advice All directors have direct access to the Chief Legal and Corporate Responsibility
Officer and the Company Secretary of the Company, who are responsible
for advising the Board on corporate governance, ESG, legal and compliance
matters.
Written procedures are in place for directors to seek, at the Company’s expense,
independent professional advice in performing directors’ duties.
Access to information All directors are provided with a tablet and/or a notebook to gain access
meeting materials of the Board and Board Committee meetings through an
electronic platform.
Communication with
senior management
To enhance communication between directors and senior management
and understand management planning and strategy of the Group, directors
and senior management are invited to attend Lenovo’s GLT Meeting and
various continuous development activities with active discussion and close
collaboration among participants. Senior management are also invited to
attend Board meetings to report on relevant corporate and strategic matters
of the Group to the Board and provide additional information on reporting
items whenever necessary.

During FY2025/26, six Board meetings were held,
comprising four regular Board meetings primarily to
review quarterly business performance and strategy
execution, and two Board meetings focused on reviewing
corporate and business strategies, and other key topics
significantly influencing the Group’s development. As a
global technology company, we held Board meetings in
hybrid format, with four meetings held in-person in Hong
Kong S.A.R. of China and San Francisco, the United States.

Directors are expected to attend all meetings of the Board
and the Board Committees on which they serve, and to
devote sufficient time to the Company to perform their
duties. Where directors are unable to attend a meeting,
they receive the meeting papers and are given the
opportunity to raise any issues with the chairmen of the
meetings prior to the meetings.
At each regular Board meeting, the Board receives
updates from the CEO and the CFO on the financial and
operational performance of the Group and any specific
developments in their areas of the businesses for which
they are directly responsible and of which the Board
should be aware. The chairmen of the respective Board
Committees would also report on matters discussed and/
or approved at the relevant Board Committee meetings
held prior to the Board meetings. Meetings are structured
so as to allow sufficient consideration and discussion of all
matters.

Key responsibilities

•	Provide	leadership	and	overall	direction	for

Audit Committee
Key responsibilities
•	Assist	the	Board	in	carrying	out	its	oversight

Governance Committee

non-executive directors
The Board may also establish ad hoc committees to
approve specific projects as needed. When required,
the Board will form an independent board committee
comprising the independent non-executive directors to
review and approve transactions that normally require
Board approval.

sufficient resources to perform their duties. The Board

including their decisions and recommendations. The
member list of the Board Committees is also posted on
the Group’s website and HKEx’s website.

Corporate governance report
Board and Board Committees’ effectiveness review
The Board recognises the importance of continually assessing its performance in supporting the leadership of the
Group. The Board has a formal process, supported by the Nomination and Governance Committee and led by the Lead
Independent Director, to evaluate the performance of the Board and Board Committees, and ensure that they continue
to operate effectively and efficiently and to fulfill their respective duties. The process involves periodic evaluation of the
Board and Board Committees, consideration of succession planning, and re-election of directors by shareholders.
Board Evaluation
Re-election by Shareholders
Succession Planning
Board Evaluation
A formal Board evaluation process is in place and is conducted on a biennial basis to support continuous improvement
and to ensure that the Board and its committees continue to operate effectively and discharge their respective
responsibilities.
In FY2025/26, the Board conducted a formal evaluation, which provided a structured opportunity to assess its overall
effectiveness, identify areas of strength and improvement, and enhance its collective contribution going forward.
Oversight and Responsibilities
The Board evaluation process is led by the Lead Independent Director, with support of the Chief Legal and Corporate
Responsibility Officer and the Company Secretary. The Nomination and Governance Committee oversees of the process
and reviews the evaluation outcomes as part of its responsibilities for Board effectiveness and corporate governance.
Methodology and scope
In FY2025/26, the evaluation was conducted internally and was primarily questionnaire based. A structured and
confidential electronic questionnaire was circulated to all directors to assess the performance and effectiveness of the
Board and its committees. All anonymous responses were handled on a confidential basis.
The evaluation focused on the following areas:
•	 Board	role	and	responsibilities
•	 Board	culture
•	 Board	and	committee	composition	and	skills
•	 Board	conduct,	practices,	processes	and	effectiveness
•	 Board	contributions
•	 Strategic	and	operational	oversight
•	 Continuous	professional	development
•	 Risk	management	and	internal	control
•	 Relationship	with	senior	management
•	 Shareholders	and	stakeholders	engagement

FY2025/26 key outcomes and follow-up focus areas
Through the FY2025/26 Board evaluation process, which covered all directors and included input from key stakeholders,
including shareholders, senior executives and auditors, the Board confirmed strong overall effectiveness and concluded
that the current internal evaluation process remains effective at this stage. The evaluation also identified certain areas
for further governance enhancement, including succession planning, capability development and Board-management
interaction and Board meeting conduct. Corresponding measures have been proposed and considered by the
Nomination and Governance Committee for recommendation to the Board as part of the Board’s ongoing efforts to
enhance effectiveness.
Succession planning
The Board is ultimately responsible for succession planning for directorships and key management roles. With support
of the Nomination and Governance Committee, the structure, size, composition and succession planning are regularly
reviewed to ensure that the successors for key roles are identified and their performance is assessed. The Board
considers and approves the appointment of Board and Board Committee members based on the recommendations
from the Nomination and Governance Committee. The Board is satisfied that an appropriate plan has been in place for
orderly succession to the Board supporting its continued effectiveness and the successful discharge of its responsibilities.
Re-election by shareholders
Pursuant to the Articles of Association, one-third of the directors at the office shall retire from office at each annual
general meeting and are eligible for re-election. Any new appointments either to fill a casual vacancy or as an addition to
the Board are also subject to re-election by shareholders at the next following annual general meeting of the Company.
The Nomination and Governance Committee has reviewed each director proposed for re-election, taking into account
his/her performance, contribution to the Board and its committees, and continued independence, where applicable,
with reference to the outcomes of the Board evaluation process. Sufficient biographical and other information on those
directors seeking re-election are provided in the annual report and the circular to enable shareholders to make an
informed decision.

Risk management and internal control
At Lenovo, risk is defined as a potential action, event or circumstance that could positively or negatively affect the
Group’s ability to meet its business strategies and objectives. As an inherent part of doing business, risk must be properly
understood and managed to ensure the Group’s continued business success.
To support sustainable business value creation, Lenovo has adopted a comprehensive risk management and internal
control framework based on the “Three Lines of Defense” model. This framework is overseen by the Board, with support
from the Audit Committee, which monitors key business risks and evaluates the design and operating effectiveness of
the Group’s risk management and internal controls systems.

(LEC)

This risk management and internal control framework is designed to manage, rather than eliminate, the risk of failing
to achieve business objectives. Accordingly, it provides reasonable, rather than absolute, assurance against material
misstatement or loss. The framework helps improve risk-related communication with management, supports impact
assessments, and enables the coordinated implementation of mitigation measures.
While the Three Lines of Defense model defines roles and responsibilities, Lenovo also adopts the Committee of
Sponsoring Organizations of the Treadway Commission (COSO) framework to provide a structured process for
identifying, assessing, managing, and monitoring risks. This framework is comprised of the following key components:
